# Sūrah Al-Baqarah (Chapter 2) — Verse-by-Verse Tafsir

## Introduction to the Sūrah

Al-Baqarah is the longest sūrah in the Qur'an. It holds 286 verses, and it stands at the head of the Madinan period, when the community had a city, a treasury, neighbours of other faiths, and a state to run. Al-Qurṭubī records that the sūrah came down in Madinah in scattered instalments, and that the scholars counted it the first sūrah revealed there. A few of its verses belong to a much later moment. (2:281 — **“then every soul will be paid in full for what it has done, and none will be wronged”**) came down at Minā on the Day of Sacrifice during the Farewell Pilgrimage, and al-Qurṭubī notes that the verses on interest were also among the last parts of the Qur'an to arrive.

The sūrah takes its name from a story inside it. Moses told his people that God was commanding them to slaughter a cow — (2:67 — **“Allah commands you to sacrifice a cow.”**) — and they answered with so many questions that the command tightened around one particular animal. The whole exchange sits in this sūrah, and the chapter is named after it. A book named after a cow is a book at home in ordinary life: fields, debts, nursing mothers, marriage, and the animals a household lives on.

The early scholars weighed its place in the Book. Khālid ibn Maʿdān called it the pavilion of the Qur'an, al-Qurṭubī reports, and the name suits a sūrah of this size, carrying this much law and this much warning.

Its virtues are recorded in the collections, and Ibn Kathīr gathers them. Aḥmad, Muslim, At-Tirmidhī and An-Nasāʾī record Abū Hurayrah reporting the Prophet's ﷺ words: do not turn your houses into graves, for the house in which Sūrah al-Baqarah is recited is not entered by Shayṭān. At-Tirmidhī graded the report ḥasan ṣaḥīḥ. An-Nasāʾī records Ibn Masʿūd saying that Shayṭān leaves the house in which al-Baqarah is heard, and al-Ḥākim entered that report in al-Mustadrak and judged its chain authentic. Ad-Dārimī records Ibn Masʿūd's advice that ten verses recited at night keep Shayṭān from entering the house until morning: the first four verses, the Verse of the Seat, the two verses after it, and the last three. Ibn Kathīr also carries a report of Sahl ibn Saʿd, recorded by al-Ṭabarānī, Ibn Ḥibbān and Ibn Mardawayh, that everything has a hump and the hump of the Qur'an is al-Baqarah; whoever recites it in his house at night keeps Shayṭān away for three nights, and in the day for three days.

Two passages inside it stand out further. Ibn Kathīr notes that the authentic reports call the Verse of the Seat the greatest verse in the Book of Allah, and he records from Aḥmad the exchange in which the Prophet ﷺ asked ʿUbayy ibn Kaʿb which verse of Allah's Book was greatest. ʿUbayy named (2:255 — **“Allah! There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Him, the Ever-Living, All-Sustaining.”**), and the Prophet ﷺ said, may the knowledge delight you, Abū al-Mundhir. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān records the report about the sūrah's closing lines, that whoever recites them at night has them as a sufficiency for him.

The sūrah opens with three kinds of people walking three different roads. Verses 1 to 5 describe the believers. Verses 6 and 7 describe those who reject the message outright, and verses 8 to 20 describe the hypocrites, who say one thing and mean another. Mujāhid counted them exactly that way — four verses, two verses, thirteen verses — and Ibn Kathīr records his statement. From there the sūrah turns to humanity at large, then to the Children of Israel and the long account of their covenant, and then to the laws that shaped a community: fasting, pilgrimage, marriage, trade, loans, interest, and the rules of war. It closes with the prayer of the believers and the answer to it.

One thread ties this sūrah to the one before it. Al-Fātiḥah ends with a request: (1:6 — **“Guide us along the Straight Path”**). Al-Baqarah opens by putting down a Book and calling it a guide. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān reads the two as question and answer — a community asking to be shown the road, and a Lord answering with the Book that shows it.

---

## Verse 2:1

> Alif-Lãm-Mĩm

**THE LETTERS THAT OPEN TWENTY-NINE SŪRAHS**

The sūrah begins with three letters set side by side: ***“Alif-Lãm-Mĩm”***. They belong to a family the scholars call al-muqaṭṭaʿāt, the cut letters. They are pronounced one by one, each with its own name, and nothing joins them into a word. Twenty-nine sūrahs open this way, as Abū Bakr al-Jazāʾirī counts them: this sūrah first, and Sūrah al-Qalam, which opens with Nūn, last. Some openings are a single letter, such as Ṣād and Qāf. Some are two, such as Ṭā-Hā and Yā-Sīn. Some run to three, four or five, such as Alif-Lãm-Mĩm-Ṣād and Kāf-Hā-Yā-ʿAyn-Ṣād.

No explanation of these letters reaches us from the Prophet ﷺ, and al-Jazāʾirī says plainly that nothing on their meaning is established from him. Al-Jalālayn settles the matter in a few words: God knows best what He means by these letters. Al-Saʿdī takes the same road and gives the rule behind it. The safest thing is silence about a meaning we have no revealed basis for, joined to a firm belief that God did not send these letters as play. They carry a wisdom we have not been handed, and the Muslim rests there.

**WHAT THE EARLY SCHOLARS MADE OF THEM**

The early scholars still gathered the possibilities, and al-Ṭabarī sets them down with their chains. Qatādah said the letters are a name of the Qur'an, and Mujāhid and Ibn Jurayj are recorded saying the same. Others called them openings with which God opens His Book. Ibn Kathīr reports that Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, ʿAlī and Ibn Masʿūd held that their knowledge stays with God, and that some scholars said they are the names of the sūrahs they begin, as al-Saʿdī also reports.

One further view comes from the linguists, and al-Ṭabarī reports it as well. The letters are simply the letters of the alphabet, named one by one, the way a father says that his son recites alif, bā, tā — meaning the whole alphabet even though he stops early. Al-Kashshāf adds the small point that fixes the recitation. These are names, like the names of numbers, so they are said by their names. A governing word in front of them can carry them into a case, and otherwise they stand still.

Two sayings from the first generation appear in nearly every book on the subject. Abū Bakr is reported to have said that every book has a mystery, and that the mystery of the Qur'an lies in the openings of its sūrahs. ʿAlī is reported to have said that every book has a quintessence, and that the quintessence of this Book is the letters of spelling. Al-Baghawī records both. Ibn Kathīr counts the letters without repetition and reaches fourteen: alif, lām, mīm, ṣād, rā, kāf, hā, yā, ʿayn, ṭā, sīn, ḥā, qāf, nūn. And the scholars agree on one thing. God did not reveal them as a game. Anyone who says that part of the Qur'an means nothing has made a heavy mistake, and the verse that answers such talk is (3:7 — **“We believe in this ˹Quran˺—it is all from our Lord.”**).

**WHY THEY STAND IN FRONT OF A BOOK**

The purpose the later scholars settled on is the one al-Mukhtaṣar states in a single line. The letters point to the miracle of a Book built from the same letters people already speak with, which is why an opening like this is usually followed by a verse about the Qur'an — and here it is 2:2. Ibn Kathīr traces that view through the books. Al-Rāzī related it to al-Mubarrid, al-Qurṭubī related it to al-Farrāʾ and Quṭrub, and al-Zamakhsharī adopted it.

Al-Zamakhsharī explains why the challenge arrives in pieces of one, two, three, four and five letters, and his reason is simple. Human speech is built from such pieces, so the challenge was repeated in every size the doubters used. Abu Bakr al-Jazāʾirī adds a second benefit. The leaders of Makkah were working to stop the Qur'an from being heard, and this odd opening caught their ears; once they listened, the words reached them. Al-Baghawī adds the plainest benefit of the three. The letters ask one thing from us, and that is to believe them and recite them exactly as they came down, which is how the Companions treated them, as al-Qurṭubī also notes.

Picture a builder who has finished a house that has the whole town talking. On the morning of the argument he tips a bag onto the table: a pile of ordinary clay bricks, the kind anyone can dig from the riverbank. Then he says, you have these too. Build it. That is the shape of this opening. The alphabet is the bricks, and the claim is the building.

---
## Verse 2:2

> This is the Book! There is no doubt about it—a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺

**A POINTER TO THE BOOK THAT WAS ASKED FOR**

The sūrah puts a Book on the table and speaks about it from a distance: ***“This is the Book!”*** The Arabic opens with a pointer used for something far off, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān notices the oddity at once. The Book is right in front of the listener, so why push it away with the far pointer? Al-Ṭabarī records that most of the exegetes read the two words as carrying one sense, and he defends them with a habit of Arabic speech. Once words have been spoken, they belong to the past, and Arabic will point at something just mentioned the way it points at something absent. A man hears his friend describe an event and answers, that is exactly what you said. The talk about the letters has only just ended, so the Book arrives as what was just named.

Al-Ṭabarī offers a second reading of the same words: that the Book may mean the sūrahs revealed before this one, since they belong to the one Book. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān gives the reading that makes the whole chapter click. The pointer reaches past the letters, back to the end of al-Fātiḥah, to (1:6 — **“Guide us along the Straight Path”**). The Muslim asked for the road, and the Book that was asked for is here.

**THE DOUBT THE BOOK LEAVES NO ROOM FOR**

The next words deny doubt about the Book: ***“There is no doubt about it”***. The earliest generations were of one voice on their sense. Ibn Kathīr gathers the names: Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd, Abū al-Dardāʾ, Mujāhid, Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, ʿAṭāʾ, Qatādah and others, all reading it as a statement that the Book is from God without doubt. Ibn Abī Ḥātim adds that he knows of no disagreement on it. Al-Mukhtaṣar sharpens the point: there is nothing of doubt in it, neither in where it came from nor in what it says.

Al-Saʿdī draws a rule out of the wording that is worth keeping. When the Qur'an denies something in order to praise, the denial carries its opposite along with it. A bare absence is not praise. A house free of sickness is not praised unless it is full of health. So the denial of doubt here carries certainty, and the Book holds the clear knowledge that removes doubt. Certainty is not a side matter either, because guidance does not arrive without it, which is why the next words speak of guidance.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān sorts doubt into two kinds. The claim itself may be false, or the listener may have misread it. The Book answers the first kind with an invitation to test it: (2:23 — **“And if you are in doubt about what We have revealed to Our servant, then produce a sûrah like it and call your helpers other than Allah, if what you say is true”**). The second kind is answered by teaching, because a person who doubts out of confusion needs the explanation this sūrah spends its length supplying. The same claim appears elsewhere in shorter form: (32:2 — **“The revelation of this Book is—beyond doubt—from the Lord of all worlds”**).

**WHY THE GUIDE GUIDES SOME READERS AND NOT OTHERS**

The verse ends by naming what the Book does and for whom, in the words ***“a guide for those mindful ˹of Allah˺”***. Al-Ṭabarī takes the word for guidance from its root: to show a man the road, point him to it and make it clear. He records al-Shaʿbī's gloss, guidance out of error, and the report of Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd that the Book is light for the mindful. Then he faces the question the wording raises. If the Book is guidance, why is it guidance for the mindful alone? His answer is worth reading twice. The Book is light for the mindful, a healing for the hearts of believers, a heaviness in the ears of those who deny it, blindness for those who refuse it, and a binding proof against those who reject it. One book, and different results, because the state of the reader is not the same.

Ibn Kathīr records how the early community defined the mindfulness the verse asks for. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb asked Ubayy ibn Kaʿb what taqwā was, and Ubayy asked him whether he had ever walked a path lined with thorns. ʿUmar said he had. Ubayy asked what he did then. ʿUmar said he rolled up his sleeves and picked his way with care. Ubayy said, that is taqwā. Ibn Kathīr calls the root of the word the act of guarding yourself from what you dislike, and he supports the picture from the Qur'an: (90:10 — **“and shown them the two ways ˹of right and wrong˺”**).

Think of a lamp in a room. It does not choose who sees by it; it lights the room the same way for everyone. What decides whether a man finds the door is whether his eyes are open and whether he turned toward it. Maʿārif al-Qurʾān counts three levels of guidance. Every creature receives the first level simply by being made and directed to what it needs. The second is offered to people who can choose, and the third is given to those who act on what they are shown. This verse promises the last two to the person who guards himself, as al-Saʿdī also reads the sentence.

---
## Verse 2:3

> who believe in the unseen, establish prayer, and donate from what We have provided for them

**FAITH THAT STARTS WHERE THE EYES STOP**

The sūrah has described three kinds of people, and it turns to the first of them with the words ***“who believe in the unseen, establish prayer”***. Al-Ṭabarī fills the phrase with reports, and his witnesses do not give one definition. Ibn ʿAbbās said the unseen is what comes from God, meaning the revelation itself. A report carried from Ibn ʿAbbās and Ibn Masʿūd, along with other Companions, names Paradise, the Fire, and everything God mentioned in the Qur'an, and adds a sharp observation: the believers among the Arabs did not take this belief from a book they already owned, or from knowledge they had inherited.

Zirr ibn Ḥubaysh, a Successor, said the unseen is the Qur'an. Qatādah listed Paradise, the Fire, the raising of the dead and the Day of Judgement. Al-Rabīʿ ibn Anas included God, His angels, His messengers, the Last Day with its garden and its fire, the meeting with Him, and life after death. Al-Ṭabarī then gives the root meaning that holds all of these together: the unseen is every thing hidden from you, and he settles on the reading that faith is belief with the heart, speech with the tongue and work with the limbs.

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān draws out why this phrase sits at the head of the list. If a man tells you the cloth in front of you is black and you agree, that is confirmation, not faith, because you agreed on what your own eyes handed you. Faith begins where the eyes stop. It is accepting a report with full trust in the one who gave it, without demanding to see it first. That is why the first thing the Book says about the people it guides is not how often they pray or how much they give. It is that they trust the unseen.

**A PRAYER THAT IS ESTABLISHED, NOT VISITED**

The verb in the verse is not do the prayer; it is establish it, and the early scholars explained the difference. Ibn ʿAbbās said it means to perform the prayer with everything that goes with it. Al-Ḍaḥḥāk, reporting from him, said it means to complete the bowing, the prostration, the recitation, the humility and the attending. Qatādah said it means to guard the times, the washing, the bowing and the prostration.

Muqātil ibn Ḥayyān said it means to keep the time, finish the purity the prayer needs, and complete its bowing, prostration, recitation, witness and blessings. Al-Qurṭubī records the same concern from the early community in other words and ties it to the Book's own command elsewhere: (23:2 — **“those who are humble in their prayers”**). A body standing in prayer while the mind wanders through the day is doing something; establishing the prayer is another matter entirely.

**GIVING OUT OF WHAT IS IN THE HAND**

The third quality is ***“and donate from what We have provided for them”***, and the phrase begins with from, a preposition that carries weight. The Muslim gives out of what he holds, not the whole of it. The early commentators differed about which giving was meant first, and al-Ṭabarī sets out the reports. Ibn ʿAbbās called it the obligatory alms on wealth. A report from Ibn ʿAbbās, Ibn Masʿūd and other Companions says it was a man's spending on his own family, before the law of alms came down.

Al-Ḍaḥḥāk, in a report Ibn Kathīr carries, said it meant giving freely according to what a person could manage, until the rules of charity arrived later in the Qur'an. Ibn Kathīr notices how often the Book joins prayer and giving in one breath, and gives the reason: prayer is the right of God, and giving is the right of His creatures, so the two together cover a person's duty in both directions.

The three qualities climb in a deliberate order, from the inward to the outward. Belief in the unseen is hidden inside a man, where no neighbour can see it. Prayer is half hidden and half visible: a body stands and bows, and only God knows what the heart brought. Giving leaves the pocket and enters the street, where the poor can see it and the ledger can record it. Think of a sailor steering by a star he cannot touch. He is not being foolish; the star has never failed anyone who steered by it. The believer steers his life by things he cannot see, and the two qualities that follow make that trust visible: a body that stands in prayer, and a hand that gives what it holds.

---
## Verse 2:4

> and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ and what was revealed before you, and have sure faith in the Hereafter

**FAITH THAT TAKES EVERY MESSENGER**

The fourth verse adds two qualities to the portrait, and the first is ***“and who believe in what has been revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺”***. Ibn ʿAbbās gives the sense in a sentence: they believe in what Allah sent you with, and in what the earlier messengers were sent with; they do not split one messenger from another, and they reject nothing that came from their Lord. Al-Mukhtaṣar says the same in fewer words: without distinction. A believer does not pick a favourite prophet and discard the rest, because the messages come from one sender and the messengers spoke for one Lord.

The Book states the same principle in the sūrah of the Women, where the command is to declare belief in God, in what was revealed to the Messenger, and in what was revealed to Abraham, Ishmael, Isaac, Jacob and the tribes, and to make no difference between any of them: (4:152 — **“And those who believe in Allah and His messengers and make no distinction between any of them—He will soon give them their rewards.”**). Faith in the last message carries faith in every message that led to it, which is why the community is asked to state both in one breath.

**THE SCRIPTURES THAT CAME BEFORE**

The second quality is ***“and what was revealed before you”***, and the line raises a question the commentators took seriously. Does the description continue the believers of the previous verse, or does it introduce a second group? Al-Ṭabarī carries a report from al-Suddī that the two groups are the believers among the Arabs and the believers among the People of the Book, who had followed the earlier scriptures and then accepted the Prophet ﷺ, and that the last verse of the passage gathers both.

Ibn Kathīr takes the wider reading, and the Book supports it. The believers are told elsewhere that the Qur'an confirms what came before it and guards it, so that the earlier revelation is not discarded but carried forward and protected: (5:48 — **“And We have revealed to you ˹O Prophet˺ the Book in truth, confirming what came before it and as a guardian over it.”**). This is a community that owns a library rather than a single volume, and it reads the newest book in the light of the ones before it.

**CERTAINTY ABOUT THE LIFE TO COME**

The verse closes with ***“and have sure faith in the Hereafter”***, and the words add a quality the previous verses had left unstated. The Arabic carries more than belief: it carries a settled assurance that keeps a person steady, and Ibn Kathīr reads it as the firm knowledge of what is to come, joined to action. Al-Saʿdī explains the same sense, that the person described here expects the meeting with his Lord and prepares for it.

The Book gives the reason this certainty belongs at the head of the sūrah. It is the conviction that decides how a person spends what he has, because a man who believes that accounts are settled elsewhere can give in this world without fear: (2:3 — **“and donate from what We have provided for them”**). Al-Qurṭubī notes that the Hereafter is named in the Book with more than thirty names, and that the reason is the variety of its conditions, each name describing a different stage of the day, as al-Mukhtaṣar also observes.

The four qualities of these verses form one picture, and a reader can hold them in the order the Book gave them. A heart that trusts what it cannot see, a body that stands in prayer, a hand that gives from what it holds, and a mind that counts on the meeting with its Lord. Picture a man who sets out on a long sea voyage and packs for it. He carries a compass when he cannot see the coast, he keeps a routine on deck, he shares his supplies with the others on board, and he plans every day around the port he has never visited. The port is what makes the other three possible, because a man who expects no landfall has no reason to keep watch.

---
## Verse 2:5

> It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord, and it is they who will be successful

**THE POINTER THAT GATHERS UP THE THREE QUALITIES**

The passage closes with a pointer: those, not these. Al-Kashshāf and al-Bayḍāwī explain the work the pointer does. When the Book said earlier that it is guidance for the mindful, a listener could ask why the mindful were singled out for it. The three qualities that follow — trusting the unseen, prayer, giving — are the answer, and the pointer gathers them up: ***“It is they who are ˹truly˺ guided by their Lord”***. Al-Bayḍāwī adds that the pointer is stronger than repeating the noun, because it throws the weight on the description and shows that the ruling follows from it.

Then comes the phrase that carries the promise, guidance from their Lord. Al-Bayḍāwī reads the Arabic preposition as a picture, the way a rider is upon an animal. Guidance is not something these people visit now and then; it is something they are settled on and carried by. And it comes from their Lord, not from their own cleverness. Ibn Kathīr calls it light, proof and insight, and al-Mukhtaṣar calls it the road that leads to Him.

**WHO THE PROMISE COVERS**

Al-Ṭabarī asks who the pointer covers, and his answer is generous. He reports that it gathers both groups described in the two earlier verses — the believers among the Arabs, and the believers among the People of the Book — and that the promise of guidance and success belongs to them together. Al-Ṭabarī also records two narrower readings: that the pointer covers only the mindful, or only the believers among the People of the Book. Then he returns to the grammar and shows why the argument can go either way. The words "and those who believe" can hang from the end of the first description or open a new sentence, and the closing verse settles the dispute by gathering everyone the passage has described into one promise. Difference of background does not divide the people of this Book, and the point is worth holding on to in a sūrah that will soon speak hard words to the Children of Israel.

**SUCCESS AS A FARMER'S WORD**

The second half of the verse names the outcome: ***“and it is they who will be successful”***. Al-Jalālayn spells success out plainly: they will enter Paradise and be saved from the Fire. Ibn Kathīr widens it to both lives, saying they gain what they were seeking and are kept from what they were dreading, in this world and the next. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps both halves: they gain what they desire, and they are saved from what they fear.

The Arabic word carries a picture that English leaves behind. Falāḥ means thriving and success, and the older language ties it to cultivation, to soil breaking open and a plant coming through. The call to prayer still uses the word, since after the call to prayer comes the call to falāḥ. So the sūrah's first portrait of a believer is a farmer's picture. He plants what no eye can see under the ground, and that is trust in the unseen. He waters it on time, and that is the prayer. He shares the crop, and that is the giving. The harvest is what the verse names.

The five verses just read are the measuring line of the sūrah, and the verses that follow show the people who fail it: those who reject the message outright, and those who talk like believers while living as strangers to the Book. Success in this sūrah is never a private mood or a feeling in the chest. It is a promise with a picture attached: a road that a person is settled on, a Lord who gave it, and a harvest waiting at the end of the season.

---
## Verse 2:6

> As for those who persist in disbelief, it is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe

**THE SECOND GROUP, AND THE WORD THAT DESCRIBES THEM**

The sūrah has finished describing the believers, and it now turns to a second group: ***“As for those who persist in disbelief, it is the same whether you warn them or not”***. The Arabic verb is kafara, and Ibn Kathīr gives its sense at the root — to cover something and hide it. A person who covers a truth he can see is not the same as a person who never met it.

The early scholars differed about who was meant, and al-Ṭabarī sets out the reports. Ibn ʿAbbās held that the verse came down about the Jews living near Madinah, who knew who the Prophet ﷺ was and still refused him. A second report from Ibn ʿAbbās, carried through ʿIkrimah and Saʿīd ibn Jubayr, says that the opening of this sūrah, down to its hundredth verse, concerns named rabbis and named hypocrites from the tribes of Aws and Khazraj; al-Ṭabarī leaves the names out rather than lengthen his book with them. Al-Jalālayn lists a different pair at the other end of Arabia: Abū Jahl and Abū Lahab, the two men who fought the message hardest in Makkah. The descriptions fit all of them, which is why the sūrah does not name a single person.

**WHAT WARNING STILL DOES**

The clause about warning needs care, because it can be misread into carelessness. Al-Saʿdī explains what the warning still does. For a person whose rejection has settled into a fixed quality, the call no longer changes his ending, but it still establishes God's proof against him. The messenger is not a salesman who is judged by his sales. He is judged by whether he delivered the message, and the verse is spoken to comfort him at a moment when the rejection was personal and painful. Al-Saʿdī reads the verse as cutting off the Prophet's ﷺ longing to save every single person in front of him, and he points to the same comfort elsewhere: (36:10 — **“It is the same whether you warn them or not—they will never believe”**).

The Qur'an repeats this sentence in more than one place, and the repetition has a purpose. Ibn Kathīr gathers the companions to this verse. (10:96 — **“Indeed, those against whom Allah’s decree ˹of torment˺ is justified will not believe”**), and the verse after it, (10:97 — **“even if every sign were to come to them—until they see the painful punishment”**), describe people whom no argument will reach, because their refusal is not caused by a shortage of evidence. (63:6 — **“It is the same whether you pray for their forgiveness or not, Allah will not forgive them”**) carries the same shape into the case of forgiveness.

Think of a doctor who diagnoses a patient who has already stopped listening. He still writes the prescription and still records the visit. What he has done is put the truth in the patient's hand and on the record. The medicine will not be swallowed, but nobody can later say the doctor kept quiet.

**A VERDICT ABOUT A GROUP, NOT A LICENCE**

The verse ends with ***“they will never believe”***, and Al-Jalālayn reads the ending as a statement about God's knowledge: they do not believe, and God knows that they will not, so the Prophet ﷺ should not build his hopes on their turning. The point is not that God's word turned them away from faith. The whole sūrah treats the rejection as something they chose first, and the sealing that the next verse describes is described as a consequence of that choice. Ibn Kathīr keeps both halves of the matter together and warns against a reader who pulls them apart in either direction.

The grammar adds a small detail that the study draft in tafsir_initial notes. The words can be read as a present tense statement — they do not believe — or as a future, they will not believe. Arabic carries both, and the translation's choice of the future reads the sentence as a verdict about an outcome rather than a report about a moment. The verdict belongs to a particular group whose path God has disclosed, and it is not a rule that lets a reader write off any person he happens to find difficult. When the Prophet ﷺ entered Makkah years later, the men who had led the killing became Muslims, and Abū Sufyān and others of the same generation ended their lives as believers. The verse is about the ones whose refusal is final, and only God knows which case is which, as al-Ṭabarī also observes when he refuses to fix the verse on one set of names.

---
## Verse 2:7

> Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and their sight is covered. They will suffer a tremendous punishment

**THE PICTURE A SEAL MAKES**

The next verse names the consequence of the refusal in the plainest physical terms: ***“Allah has sealed their hearts and their hearing, and their sight is covered.”*** Al-Ṭabarī opens with the shape of the word. The root of the seal means to stamp, and a stamp is pressed on a container. He then asks the question a reader wants to ask. How can a heart be sealed when a seal needs a lid or a wrapper? His answer builds the picture that makes the verse work. Hearts are containers for knowledge, and the hearing is the door by which news of the unseen arrives. Seal them shut and the contents stop changing.

He also records the disagreement about what the seal is, since the Qur'an leaves it unnamed. Al-Suddī says it means simply that God sealed them. Qatādah says the people obeyed Shayṭān first, and then God sealed the three faculties, so that none of them could reach the guidance again. Mujāhid describes it as a stamp that arrives when sin settles in a heart and surrounds it from every side. The sequence in all these reports is the same, and it runs in one direction. The sin comes first, and the seal follows. Nobody is described as sealed before he refused.

**SIN FIRST, THEN THE SEAL**

Maʿārif al-Qurʾān gathers the same point into a sentence worth remembering: one punishment for an evil deed is another evil deed that comes after it, and one reward for a good deed is another good deed that follows. He records the hadith that describes the process grown slow and visible: when a servant commits a sin a black dot appears on his heart, and each new sin adds another until the heart turns dark and can no longer see good as good. The report is carried in the Musnad of Aḥmad and the Sunan of At-Tirmidhī, as Maʿārif notes by way of Mishkāt al-Maṣābīh, and the Qur'an names that darkness: (83:14 — **“their hearts have been stained by all ˹the evil˺ they used to commit”**).

Al-Saʿdī draws the line that keeps the verse honest. The three roads of knowledge are closed by the sealing. The heart no longer takes in what would help it, the ear no longer benefits from what it hears, and the eye no longer sees what would steer it. This is a punishment, and the punishment fits the crime, because these are the same three faculties that were offered the truth and refused it. The Qur'an states the rule in one line: (6:110 — **“We turn their hearts and eyes away ˹from the truth˺ as they refused to believe at first”**).

A man who bolts every door of his house at night and then complains that no light comes in has described his own situation accurately, and this is the shape the verse draws. The doors were open while the message was being offered. What closes them is the refusal itself.

**THE PENALTY THE VERSE CLOSES ON**

The verse ends with the sentence the earlier one withheld: ***“They will suffer a tremendous punishment”***. Al-Jalālayn describes the punishment as intense and lasting, and the Arabic word carries weight rather than merely size. Notice which faculties are named in the first half and which group they belong to. The open rejecters lose the heart, the hearing and the sight — the three instruments a person needs to find the road — and then they meet a penalty that is called tremendous.

The sūrah keeps a careful vocabulary for its two groups, and the difference is worth noticing as the verses go by. The rejecters are promised a tremendous punishment, and the liars of the next passage are promised a painful one. The Book never uses the same word twice where a reader might learn something from the change. The end of the passage returns a third time with a different word again, as al-Qurṭubī notes when he compares the three.

---
## Verse 2:8

> And there are some who say, “We believe in Allah and the Last Day,” yet they are not ˹true˺ believers

**THE THIRD GROUP ARRIVES WITHOUT A NAME**

***“And there are some who say,”*** the verse begins, and the sūrah turns to its third group. It does not name them; it only says that among people there are some who talk, and the verses that follow describe them until the name arrives at 2:14. Ibn Kathīr defines the condition in one line: nifāq is to show agreement and conceal something evil. Ibn Jurayj, quoted in his commentary, gives a portrait of the person this describes. His deeds differ from what he advertises, what he hides differs from what he says, and his entrance is not like his exit.

The historical setting explains why these verses belong to Madinah and not to Makkah. Ibn Kathīr lays the story out. In Makkah there was almost the opposite problem: some people were forced to hide their faith while their hearts held it. Madinah was different. Two Arab tribes, Aws and Khazraj, had lived there in the days before Islam, alongside three Jewish tribes, and the two Arab tribes were on the edge of making ʿAbdullāh ibn Ubayy ibn Salūl their king when the message arrived and much of the city accepted Islam. After Badr, when the Muslims became strong enough to be worth reckoning with, he and others like him entered Islam with their tongues while their hearts stayed where they were. Fear, not conviction, wrote the sentence on their lips.

Al-Qurṭubī records how neatly the sūrah divides its opening peoples. Mujāhid counted four verses about the believers, two about the rejecters, and thirteen about the hypocrites, and al-Qurṭubī agrees with the count. He also records a remark of the early teachers that the word for people here is a general noun, and that the friends of God are not described with it. The sūrah spends more time on the pretenders than on the open enemies, and the length is the lesson. The community was going to live with these people, marry into their families and trade with them, so it needed to know how to read them.

**THE HALF-TRUTH THEY CHOSE**

Their statement is a half-truth, and Maʿārif al-Qurʾān picks up exactly what is missing from it, in the words ***“We believe in Allah and the Last Day”***. The men claiming to believe mention Allah and the Last Day, and the name of the Prophet ﷺ does not appear in their sentence at all. Even a group this hostile would rather avoid a specific lie than tell it. Faith in the Messenger would have been a claim about a man standing in front of them, and something in them hesitated over that. So they built a formula broad enough to sound right and vague enough to be safe, and the Book reports the same instinct in another place: (63:1 — **“When the hypocrites come to you ˹O Prophet˺, they say, ‘We bear witness that you are certainly the Messenger of Allah’”**). Al-Qurṭubī reads that later formula as the same kind of edit, a sentence chosen for what it does not say.

The sūrah gathers this kind of speech in more than one place, and the pattern repeats. (49:14 — **“˹Some of˺ the nomadic Arabs say, ‘We believe.’ Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ ‘You have not believed. But say, “We have submitted,” for faith has not yet entered your hearts.”**), where the answer separates the word from the state with one correction. The Book draws a line here that a reader should hold on to. A claim of faith is not itself faith, and the community is asked to look past the sentence to the life underneath it.

**A CLAIM THAT THE VERSE REFUSES**

The verse closes by refusing the claim outright: ***“yet they are not ˹true˺ believers”***. The Arabic states the denial with a construction that leaves no room for a polite reading, and the translation's brackets show it. They say one thing, and the verse says they are not believers at all. Al-Mukhtaṣar keeps the plain sense: some people claim faith in Allah when they do not in fact believe.

Al-Saʿdī divides the condition into two kinds so a reader can tell them apart. There is hypocrisy in belief, which removes a person from Islam altogether, and it is the condition of the men this passage is about. There is also hypocrisy in deed, which is a serious sin but leaves a person inside the community. For the second kind he describes the report the Prophet ﷺ gave about such persons: when they speak they lie, when they promise they fail, and when they are trusted they betray; in another narration, when they argue they abuse. Al-Saʿdī's care with the two categories matters, because it stops a Muslim from throwing the heaviest word in the language at anyone who disappoints him.

Both kinds share one structure, and it is worth drawing. The outside and the inside do not match. Picture a shop hanging a sign that says genuine goods, while its back room holds something else. The sign is real, the letters are painted, and the contents are unchanged by any of it. The verse is about the gap between the sign and the room, and the next verses describe what the gap costs.

## Verse 2:187

> It has been made permissible for you to be intimate with your wives during the nights preceding the fast. Your spouses are a garment for you as you are for them. Allah knows that you were deceiving yourselves. So He has accepted your repentance and pardoned you. So now you may be intimate with them and seek what Allah has prescribed for you. ˹You may˺ eat and drink until you see the light of dawn breaking the darkness of night, then complete the fast until nightfall. Do not be intimate with your spouses while you are meditating in the mosques. These are the limits set by Allah, so do not exceed them. This is how Allah makes His revelations clear to people, so they may become mindful ˹of Him˺

## Verse 2:196

> Complete the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage for Allah. But if prevented ˹from proceeding˺, then ˹offer˺ whatever sacrificial animals you can afford. And do not shave your heads until the sacrificial animal reaches its destination. But if any of you is ill or has a scalp ailment ˹requiring shaving˺, then compensate either by fasting, charity, or a sacrificial offering. In times of peace, you may combine the pilgrimage and minor pilgrimage then make the sacrificial offering you can afford. Whoever cannot afford that ˹offering˺, let them fast three days during pilgrimage and seven after returning ˹home˺—completing ten. These offerings are for those who do not live near the Sacred House. And be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is severe in punishment

## Verse 2:217

> They ask you ˹O Prophet˺ about fighting in the sacred months. Say, “Fighting during these months is a great sin. But hindering ˹others˺ from the Path of Allah, rejecting Him, and expelling the worshippers from the Sacred Mosque is ˹a˺ greater ˹sin˺ in the sight of Allah. For persecution is far worse than killing. And they will not stop fighting you until they turn you away from your faith—if they can. And whoever among you renounces their own faith and dies a disbeliever, their deeds will become void in this life and in the Hereafter. It is they who will be the residents of the Fire. They will be there forever.”

## Verse 2:233

> ˹Divorced˺ mothers will breastfeed their offspring for two whole years, for those who wish to complete the nursing ˹of their child˺. The child’s father will provide reasonable maintenance and clothing for the mother ˹during that period˺. No one will be charged with more than they can bear. No mother or father should be made to suffer for their child. The ˹father’s˺ heirs are under the same obligation. But if both sides decide—after mutual consultation and consent—to wean a child, then there is no blame on them. If you decide to have your children nursed by a wet-nurse, it is permissible as long as you pay fairly. Be mindful of Allah, and know that Allah is All-Seeing of what you do

## Verse 2:259

> Or ˹are you not aware of˺ the one who passed by a city which was in ruins. He wondered, “How could Allah bring this back to life after its destruction?” So Allah caused him to die for a hundred years then brought him back to life. Allah asked, “How long have you remained ˹in this state˺?” He replied, “Perhaps a day or part of a day.” Allah said, “No! You have remained here for a hundred years! Just look at your food and drink—they have not spoiled. ˹But now˺ look at ˹the remains of˺ your donkey! And ˹so˺ We have made you into a sign for humanity. And look at the bones ˹of the donkey˺, how We bring them together then clothe them with flesh!” When this was made clear to him, he declared, “˹Now˺ I know that Allah is Most Capable of everything.”

## Verse 2:282

> O believers! When you contract a loan for a fixed period of time, commit it to writing. Let the scribe maintain justice between the parties. The scribe should not refuse to write as Allah has taught them to write. They will write what the debtor dictates, bearing Allah in mind and not defrauding the debt. If the debtor is incompetent, weak, or unable to dictate, let their guardian dictate for them with justice. Call upon two of your men to witness. If two men cannot be found, then one man and two women of your choice will witness—so if one of the women forgets the other may remind her. The witnesses must not refuse when they are summoned. You must not be against writing ˹contracts˺ for a fixed period—whether the sum is small or great. This is more just ˹for you˺ in the sight of Allah, and more convenient to establish evidence and remove doubts. However, if you conduct an immediate transaction among yourselves, then there is no need for you to record it, but call upon witnesses when a deal is finalized. Let no harm come to the scribe or witnesses. If you do, then you have gravely exceeded ˹your limits˺. Be mindful of Allah, for Allah ˹is the One Who˺ teaches you. And Allah has ˹perfect˺ knowledge of all things

## Verse 2:286

> Allah does not require of any soul more than what it can afford. All good will be for its own benefit, and all evil will be to its own loss. ˹The believers pray,˺ “Our Lord! Do not punish us if we forget or make a mistake. Our Lord! Do not place a burden on us like the one you placed on those before us. Our Lord! Do not burden us with what we cannot bear. Pardon us, forgive us, and have mercy on us. You are our ˹only˺ Guardian. So grant us victory over the disbelieving people.”
